final answer
ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the `creation` timestamp of the Stock Ledger Entry (SLE). It does not use `posting_date` or `posting_time` alone. The cancelled voucher's own entries are handled separately. I read the code in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` but did not run it or trace the full repost flow. **1. Ordering key** Every SLE query that has to pick the previous, next or future entry orders by `posting_datetime` and then `creation`: - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` uses `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1`. - `get_stock_ledger_entries` uses `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}`. - `get_next_stock_reco` and `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty` use `.orderby(posting_datetime).orderby(creation)`. `posting_datetime` is the combined date and time. `creation` is the second tiebreaker, so a Purchase Receipt and a Material Transfer posted in the same second still have a deterministic order. **2. Selecting the "previous" SLE: `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher`** It always filters on `is_cancelled = 0` and has three modes: - **`exclude_current_voucher=True`:** it adds `voucher_no != current` and uses the operator passed in (for example `<=`). `get_stock_reco_qty_shift` uses this to get the balance before a reconciliation. - **Submit or repost with `creation` and `sle_id` set, and not cancelled:** it switches the operator to `<=` and adds `creation < <this SLE's creation>`. Entries at the same timestamp therefore count as "previous" only if they were created earlier. - **Cancelled:** the `creation` clause is skipped, so only the plain `posting_datetime` comparison applies. **3. Selecting "future" SLEs: `update_qty_in_future_sle`** The future condition is `posting_datetime > X`. For a non-cancelled entry with a `creation` value, it adds `OR (posting_datetime == X AND creation > this.creation)`. On cancellation (`is_cancelled` set), the same-timestamp clause is dropped. Only strictly later entries are shifted, and the cancelled voucher's own entries are already excluded by `is_cancelled = 0`. A same-second entry that was created earlier is not shifted, and one created later is handled by the repost. **4. Reconciliation boundaries** `get_next_reco_datetime_condition` and `get_datetime_limit_condition` use the same pattern. For a cancelled entry the condition is `posting_datetime >= current`. Otherwise it is `posting_datetime > current OR (== AND creation > current.creation)`. For limiting the recalculation range, it is `< next_reco_datetime OR (== AND creation < reco.creation)`. **5. Negative-stock validation: `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle`** It returns early if negative stock is allowed or the entry is an incoming one (`actual_qty >= 0`, unless it's a Stock Reconciliation). Otherwise it calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`. That query looks for the first SLE with: - the same item and warehouse - a different `voucher_no` - `posting_datetime >= this posting_datetime` - `is_cancelled = 0` - `qty_after_transaction < 0` It orders by `posting_datetime` then `creation` and takes `limit 1`. The cancelled voucher's own rows are excluded by `voucher_no` and `is_cancelled`. Same-second rows are included through `>=`. `qty_after_transaction` has already been recomputed in `creation` order, so the check reads a consistent running balance. If it finds a negative, it raises `NegativeStockError`. The batch version, `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty`, uses a window sum `over (order by posting_datetime, creation)`. **Summary:** Ties are broken by `creation`. Cancelled rows are ignored through `is_cancelled = 0`. The `creation <` and `creation >` clauses apply only when the entry is not being cancelled. The negative-stock check then runs against the recomputed balances in that order.

final answer
Same-timestamp ordering is settled by the Stock Ledger Entry `creation` timestamp, which acts as a tiebreaker after `posting_datetime`. This is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I read the code but didn't run any of it. **Ordering key.** `posting_date` and `posting_time` are combined by `get_combine_datetime` (`erpnext/stock/utils.py`) into the indexed `posting_datetime` column. Every ordering and comparison uses `posting_datetime` first and `creation` second. For example, `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`, and `get_next_stock_reco` orders by `posting_datetime, creation`. **Previous SLE (`get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher`).** - When the voucher is being submitted (it has `creation` and `sle_id`, and `cancelled` is false), the operator becomes `<=` and the query adds `creation < <this SLE's creation>`. A same-second entry therefore only counts as "previous" if it was created earlier. Without that `creation` filter, an SLE created after the current one could be picked up. - `exclude_current_voucher=True` adds `voucher_no != current`. `get_stock_reco_qty_shift` uses this for Stock Reconciliation. - On cancellation, `cancelled=True` skips the `creation` clause. The cancelled voucher's own SLEs are already `is_cancelled = 1` and are filtered out by `is_cancelled = 0`. The previous entry is then the latest remaining non-cancelled SLE at or before that datetime. **Current SLEs (`get_sle_against_current_voucher`, used by `process_sle_against_current_timestamp`).** - It selects non-cancelled SLEs for the item and warehouse with `posting_datetime ==` the given datetime, ordered by `creation asc`. - For a normal submit it also requires `creation == kwargs.creation`, so only the exact SLE is reposted. - For a cancel it drops that filter and reprocesses all same-timestamp SLEs in creation order, so the entries that remain are recomputed in a deterministic sequence. **Shifting future balances (`update_qty_in_future_sle`).** - The "future" condition is `posting_datetime > this` OR, on submit only, `(posting_datetime == this AND creation > this creation)`. On cancel (`is_cancelled` set) it is only `posting_datetime > this`. - The cancel case is why the later same-second entry isn't mis-ordered. The cancelled SLE is already excluded from the update by `is_cancelled = 0`. - The shift stops at the next Stock Reconciliation, using `get_next_stock_reco` and `get_datetime_limit_condition`. That limit is `posting_datetime < reco` OR `(posting_datetime == reco AND creation < reco.creation)`. `get_next_reco_datetime_condition` uses `>=` on cancel and `> OR (== AND creation >)` on submit. **Negative-stock validation.** `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle` calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`. That function looks for the first non-cancelled SLE (in `posting_datetime, creation` order) with `qty_after_transaction < 0`, `posting_datetime >=` the cancelled transaction's, and a different `voucher_no`. The `>=` is deliberate: same-timestamp entries are included instead of being skipped as ambiguous. The batch version, `get_future_sle_with_negative_batch_qty`, builds a running `sum(actual_qty) over (order by posting_datetime, creation)` and applies the same `>=` check. **Batch and serial valuation.** `BatchNoValuation.get_batch_stock_before_date` in `serial_batch_bundle.py` uses the same rule: `posting_datetime < X` OR `(== X AND creation < sle.creation)`. It also excludes the current voucher.

final answer
When two SLEs have the same `posting_datetime`, ERPNext breaks the tie with the `creation` timestamp. It also uses the cancelled flag to decide which side of that tie counts as "previous" and which as "future". All of this is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. I read the code but did not run it. **1. Posting datetime is the primary key.** - SLEs carry a combined `posting_datetime`, built by `get_combine_datetime(posting_date, posting_time)`. - Every comparison uses that column rather than separate date and time fields. **2. `creation` is the tie-breaker.** - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` and `get_stock_ledger_entries` both order by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - `get_sle_against_current_voucher` returns the entries at exactly the same datetime, ordered `creation asc`. - `sort_sles` in the repost class sorts the repost queue by `(posting_datetime, creation)`. **3. "Previous" for a live entry (`get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher`).** - If `creation`, `sle_id` and not-cancelled are all set, the operator becomes `<=` and the query adds `creation < '<this creation>'`. - So an entry at the same second is "previous" only if it was created earlier. - It also filters `is_cancelled = 0`. **4. Future entries when posting or cancelling (`update_qty_in_future_sle`).** - The future condition is `posting_datetime > X`. - For a non-cancelled entry that has a `creation`, it also includes `posting_datetime == X and creation > args.creation`. - For a cancelled entry that second clause is dropped. The condition is strictly `posting_datetime > X`, and the cancelled SLE is excluded by `is_cancelled == 0`. - The qty shift (`actual_qty`) is applied to those future rows' `qty_after_transaction`. This is how a same-second Purchase Receipt and Material Transfer stay consistently ordered after a cancellation. **5. Stock Reconciliation boundaries.** - `get_next_reco_datetime_condition` uses `>=` on datetime when cancelling. - Otherwise it uses `>` on datetime, or the same datetime with `creation >` the current one. - `get_datetime_limit_condition` bounds the update range with `posting_datetime < X or (== X and creation < reco.creation)`. **6. Negative-stock validation.** - `update_qty_in_future_sle` calls `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle`. - That function returns early if negative stock is allowed, or if `actual_qty >= 0` and the voucher isn't a Stock Reconciliation. - Otherwise `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty` finds a later SLE (`voucher_no != current`, `posting_datetime >= X`) whose recomputed `qty_after_transaction` is negative. - `is_negative_with_precision` checks it, and `NegativeStockError` is thrown if so. - Because the balances were recomputed with the deterministic `(posting_datetime, creation)` order, the check does not depend on which same-second row the database happens to return first. **7. Repost.** - `RepostItemValuation`'s reposting loop pops entries from a deque sorted by `sort_sles`. - It seeds `prev_sle_dict` with `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher(sle)`. - It processes each SLE in `process_sle`, taking the previous SLE through the same creation-aware lookup.

final answer
**Short answer:** ERPNext breaks same-timestamp ties with the Stock Ledger Entry's `creation` column. Every ordering and comparison uses the pair `(posting_datetime, creation)`, never `posting_datetime` alone. On cancellation the "current" and "previous" sets are chosen differently from submission. All the code is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`. **1. The sort key** `posting_date` and `posting_time` are combined into one `posting_datetime` column via `get_combine_datetime`. Wherever entries are ordered, `creation` is added as the secondary key: - `get_stock_ledger_entries` uses `order by posting_datetime {order}, creation {order}` and filters `is_cancelled = 0`. `get_previous_sle` calls it with `<=`, `desc` and `limit 1`. - `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` uses `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1 ... for update`. - `get_sle_against_current_voucher` uses `orderby(creation asc)`. - `get_next_stock_reco` orders by `posting_datetime` then `creation`. - `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty` also orders by `posting_datetime` then `creation`. **2. Posting a stock transaction** `make_sl_entries` creates the SLE and calls `repost_current_voucher`. That builds `update_entries_after`, passing `sle_id`, `creation` and `cancelled`. - **Current entries.** `build()` calls `process_sle_against_current_timestamp`, which calls `get_sle_against_current_voucher`. It selects entries with the same item, warehouse and `posting_datetime`, and `is_cancelled = 0`. When the voucher is not being cancelled it adds `creation == kwargs.creation`. That pins "current" to exactly this SLE, even if the Purchase Receipt and Material Transfer share a timestamp. - **Previous entry.** `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` takes the `sle_id` and `creation` branch. It switches the operator to `<=` and adds `and creation < '<this creation>'`. The previous entry is the latest one at the same timestamp that was created earlier, so the same-second sibling is correctly treated as prior or later. - **Future balances.** `update_qty_in_future_sle` treats an entry as "future" if `posting_datetime > X`. It also counts `posting_datetime == X and creation > args.creation`, but only when the entry is not cancelled. **3. Cancelling** - `make_sl_entries` first runs `set_as_cancel`, which marks the voucher's SLEs `is_cancelled = 1`. It then writes reversal entries with `actual_qty` negated. - **Current entries on cancel.** `get_sle_against_current_voucher` skips the `creation ==` filter when `cancelled` is true. It returns all live SLEs at that exact `posting_datetime`, ordered by `creation asc`. The reposting therefore reprocesses every same-timestamp survivor in creation order, not only the cancelled entry's slot. - **Previous entry on cancel.** `get_previous_sle_of_current_voucher` skips the `creation <` branch when `cancelled` is true. It uses the strict `posting_datetime < X`, so the previous entry is the last one in an earlier time bucket. The same-timestamp survivors are then replayed in `creation` order starting from that balance. - **Future entries.** `update_qty_in_future_sle` skips the `creation > …` clause when `is_cancelled` is set, so every entry at or after the timestamp is shifted. **4. Negative-stock validation** - **Per-entry check.** `process_sle` loads `prev_sle_dict[(item, warehouse)]` from that previous entry. When negative stock isn't allowed (or the item is serialized), it calls `validate_negative_stock`. `validate_previous_sle_qty` warns when the previous balance is negative and the entry is inbound. - **Future-balance check.** `validate_negative_qty_in_future_sle` runs after `update_qty_in_future_sle`. It calls `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`, which finds the first live SLE with `qty_after_transaction < 0`. That query matches `posting_datetime >= X`, excludes the same `voucher_no`, requires `is_cancelled = 0`, and orders by `posting_datetime, creation`. If it finds one, it raises `NegativeStockError` ("units of … needed in … to complete this transaction"). - **Why same-second ties don't fool it.** The balances checked are `qty_after_transaction` values recomputed in creation order. The cancelled voucher's own SLEs are excluded by `is_cancelled`, and `creation` fixes which sibling comes first. **5. Full reposts** For a Repost Item Valuation, `repost_future_sle` passes `creation` into `update_entries_after` for each item-warehouse. `get_future_entries_to_repost` and `get_sle_after_datetime` call `get_stock_ledger_entries` with `>=` or `>` and `asc`, so they also sort by `posting_datetime, creation`. **Caveat** `creation` is the tiebreaker, not a logical dependency order. If a transfer's outgoing SLE has a `creation` earlier than the receipt that supplies its stock, it is treated as earlier and can still hit negative stock. Several functions (`get_sle_against_current_voucher`, `get_future_sle_with_negative_qty`) rely on `creation` ordering alone for that reason.
Q18Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesWhen a Purchase Receipt is created for an internal (intra-company) stock transfer, how does the system determine the item's incomi…
When a Purchase Receipt is created for an internal (intra-company) stock transfer, how does the system determine the item's incoming rate, and does it use a value that was fixed at the time the source Delivery Note was made or one computed fresh at receipt time?

final answer
The Purchase Receipt (PR) uses the incoming rate that the source Delivery Note (DN) stored on its item row. It doesn't compute a fresh rate at receipt time. I didn't open the DN-side write-back code in full, so that part rests on one grep hit. **How the rate is found** - The logic is in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, in `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)`. Its docstring says the incoming rate is "the average of the outgoing rate". - The PR Item row links back to the DN through its `delivery_note_item` field. For a Purchase Invoice, the link is `sales_invoice_item`, which points to a Sales Invoice Item. - The function reads `incoming_rate` from that linked `Delivery Note Item` row. - If the PR Item has a `landed_cost_voucher_amount`, the function adds `lcv_amount / abs(actual_qty)` to that rate. So the receipt rate is the DN's stored rate plus any landed cost per unit. **When it is applied** - This happens in `update_stock_ledger` (around lines 1032–1044), the method that processes each stock ledger entry (SLE). - It applies when the voucher is a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice, it has a `voucher_detail_no`, and `is_internal_transfer(sle)` is true. That check requires `is_internal_supplier` and `represents_company == company`. - For an inward SLE, if the rate is non-zero, `sle.incoming_rate` is set to it. For an outward SLE on the same voucher, `sle.outgoing_rate` is set to it. This overrides any rate the PR would otherwise get from its own `valuation_rate`. - The same helper is called again around line 1472, in the rate lookup for the SLE path. **Fixed or fresh** - The rate is fixed at the DN. When the DN's stock ledger entries are posted, the outgoing valuation rate is written back to the DN item's `incoming_rate`. I saw this in the `incoming_rate` update around lines 1563–1570 of the same file. - The PR only reads that stored value. It does not recompute from the receiving warehouse's current valuation. - The only thing added at receipt time is the landed cost voucher amount. - A code comment says the purpose is to keep both sides of the transfer at the same rate. Otherwise a stale PR `valuation_rate` could leak the difference into COGS (cost of goods sold) as a divisional loss.

final answer
The incoming rate comes from the Delivery Note's stored value. It is not recomputed at receipt time. **Document level: `BuyingController.set_incoming_rate` in `erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py`** - This applies to a Purchase Receipt or a stock-updating Purchase Invoice that is an internal transfer and is not a return. - It calls `set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer`. - If the PR item is linked to a Delivery Note Item (`delivery_note_item`), `sales_incoming_rate` is read from that row. The field is `incoming_rate` when `is_internal_supplier` is set, otherwise `rate`. The value is multiplied by `conversion_factor`. - If the PR item has no linked Delivery Note Item, the rate is computed fresh. `stock.utils.get_incoming_rate` is called against `from_warehouse` at the PR's posting date and time with a negative quantity. That is the source warehouse's valuation at that moment. - Unless Stock Settings has `allow_internal_transfer_at_arms_length_price` enabled, the item `rate` is overwritten with `sales_incoming_rate`. Discounts and margins are zeroed, and the user sees the message "Item rate has been updated as per valuation rate since its an internal stock transfer." **Stock ledger level: `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`** - It is called from `update_entries_after.get_incoming_outgoing_rate_from_transaction` when the voucher is a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice and `is_internal_transfer(sle)` is true. - It finds the linked `delivery_note_item` (or `sales_invoice_item`) and returns that row's stored `incoming_rate`. That is the outgoing valuation rate recorded when the Delivery Note was submitted. - It adds the item's `landed_cost_voucher_amount` divided by `abs(actual_qty)`, so landed costs on the PR raise the rate. **Conclusion:** the Purchase Receipt's stock ledger entry is anchored to the Delivery Note Item's `incoming_rate`, which was fixed when the Delivery Note was made. The test `test_internal_transfer_pr_incoming_sle_anchored_to_dn_rate` shows this. Even when the PR item's `valuation_rate` is corrupted to 80 and `sales_incoming_rate` is set to 0, reposting restores the SLE rate to the Delivery Note's 100. The fresh calculation applies only when no Delivery Note link exists.

final answer
For an internal transfer, the Purchase Receipt takes its rate from the Delivery Note Item's stored value when the receipt is linked to a Delivery Note. It computes the rate fresh only when there is no such link. I read this in the code and didn't run it. The logic is in `BuyingController.set_incoming_rate()` and `set_sales_incoming_rate_for_internal_transfer()`, both in `erpnext/controllers/buying_controller.py`. **When it applies:** `set_incoming_rate()` returns early unless the document is a Purchase Receipt (or a Purchase Invoice with `update_stock`). It also returns early for returns and for anything that isn't an internal transfer. **Linked to a Delivery Note (the normal case):** If the item row has a `delivery_note_item` reference, the rate is read from the database with `frappe.db.get_value("Delivery Note Item", <ref>, field)`. The field is `incoming_rate` when `is_internal_supplier` is set, and `rate` otherwise. The value is multiplied by the row's `conversion_factor` and stored in `d.sales_incoming_rate`. That `incoming_rate` is the valuation rate stamped on the Delivery Note Item when the Delivery Note was submitted. The Purchase Receipt does not recompute it at receipt time. A test in `test_purchase_receipt.py` (around line 1913) checks this: the internal-transfer receipt's inward stock ledger entry must use the Delivery Note's `incoming_rate`. **Not linked to a Delivery Note:** The code calls `get_incoming_rate()` from `erpnext.stock.utils`. It passes the `from_warehouse`, the receipt's posting date and time, and a negative `stock_qty`. That gives the source warehouse's valuation rate as of the receipt's own posting datetime, which is a fresh calculation. It is again multiplied by `conversion_factor`. **What happens to the rate:** - Unless Stock Settings has `allow_internal_transfer_at_arms_length_price` enabled, each row's discount, discount amount and margin are zeroed. The row's `rate` is then overwritten with `sales_incoming_rate`, and the user sees a message saying so. - If that setting is enabled, the function returns before overwriting `rate`, so the receipt keeps its own price. - The stored `sales_incoming_rate` still feeds `update_valuation_rate()`, around lines 465 and 529 of the same file, which computes the item's valuation rate and the stock ledger entry's `incoming_rate`.

final answer
The rate is anchored to a value stored on the Delivery Note, not recomputed at receipt time. That stored value can still change if the Delivery Note's own valuation is reposted, and landed cost is added at receipt time. I traced this in the code but did not run it. **Where the rate is set.** The logic is in `StockReservationEntry`'s sibling, the stock ledger's `process_sle` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` (around line 1032). For a Purchase Receipt or Purchase Invoice row that has a `voucher_detail_no`, it calls `is_internal_transfer(sle)`. That check is true when `is_internal_supplier` is set and `represents_company == company`. It then calls `get_incoming_rate_for_inter_company_transfer(sle)` (line 2540). **What that function does.** 1. It follows the receipt item's `delivery_note_item` link back to the source Delivery Note Item. For a Purchase Invoice it uses `sales_invoice_item` and Sales Invoice Item instead. 2. It reads that item's stored `incoming_rate`. 3. It adds a landed-cost share, `landed_cost_voucher_amount / abs(actual_qty)`, taken from the receipt's own item row. 4. It returns the sum. The function's docstring says the incoming rate is "the average of the outgoing rate". **How the result is applied.** If the stock ledger entry (SLE) has a negative quantity, the rate becomes its `outgoing_rate`. Otherwise, if the rate is non-zero, it overwrites the SLE's `incoming_rate`. This overrides whatever the generic dynamic-rate step (`get_dynamic_incoming_outgoing_rate`) produced. A code comment explains why. Without the override, an inward SLE that inherits a stale Purchase Receipt `valuation_rate` leaks the gap into COGS. **Where the Delivery Note's rate comes from.** In `erpnext/controllers/selling_controller.py`, `set_incoming_rate` (around line 515) fills `incoming_rate` on the Delivery Note item. It calls `get_incoming_rate`, which values the item at the source warehouse on the Delivery Note's posting date and time. The Purchase Receipt therefore reads that stored field and does not recompute the rate from the target warehouse's own stock. The one exception is a repost: when the Delivery Note's valuation is reposted, its `incoming_rate` can be updated, and later receipts pick up the new value.
Q19Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesWhen a Purchase Invoice is created against a Standard Cost item's Purchase Receipt with "Update Stock" switched off, what GL entri…
When a Purchase Invoice is created against a Standard Cost item's Purchase Receipt with "Update Stock" switched off, what GL entries does the invoice post for the item's expense side, and how is that different from an invoice with Update Stock enabled?

final answer
With Update Stock off, the Purchase Invoice posts only one expense-side debit per item. It books no Purchase Price Variance (PPV). **Update Stock OFF (invoice linked to the Purchase Receipt)** The code is `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. The `get_gl_entries` method in `purchase_invoice.py` delegates to it. - The condition `doc.update_stock and doc.auto_accounting_for_stock and item in stock_items` is false, so the item goes to the `else` branch (around lines 319–350). - That branch posts one debit to `item.expense_account`, against the supplier, for the full billed amount (`base_amount` from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`). The offsetting credit goes to the supplier's payable account. - For a stock item linked to a Purchase Receipt, `item.expense_account` is the Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB) account. The debit therefore clears the SRBNB credit that the Purchase Receipt booked. - A code comment in that branch says the Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the PPV. It also says booking PPV again in the invoice would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. - This branch does not call `make_stock_adjustment_entry`, so no variance account is touched. - Two optional extras can appear here: - A provisional-accounting entry, from `make_provisional_gl_entry`. - An exchange-gain/loss pair, when the invoice's `conversion_rate` differs from the Purchase Receipt's. **Update Stock ON** - The invoice creates its own Stock Ledger Entries, so the stock-item branch (lines 180–317) applies. - `make_stock_adjustment_entry` computes `warehouse_debit_amount = valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. For a Standard Cost item, the valuation rate is the standard rate. - The invoice debits `item.expense_account` for that valuation-based `warehouse_debit_amount`, not the billed amount. The Stock In Hand account is debited through the Stock Ledger-driven entries. - If the standard value differs from the actual Stock Ledger value (`voucher_wise_stock_value`, keyed by item row and warehouse), `make_stock_adjustment_entry` adds a debit or credit to the stock variance account. That account comes from `get_stock_variance_account`, which is where PPV shows up. - It can also post landed-cost credits per account and a credit to the supplier warehouse account for sub-contracted raw material cost (`rm_supp_cost`). **Difference** - With Update Stock off, the invoice only clears SRBNB and credits the supplier, at the billed amount. The Purchase Receipt has already carried the stock and PPV. - With Update Stock on, the invoice books the stock itself at the valuation (standard) amount. Any difference from the billed or actual amount goes to the variance account. I did not look at how the Purchase Receipt itself posts the PPV, so that part rests on the code comment.

final answer
**Update Stock off.** The invoice posts one expense-side entry per item, and it posts no Purchase Price Variance (PPV). - It debits `item.expense_account`, which on a purchase-receipt-linked invoice is normally Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB). The debit is the full billed `base_amount`, with the supplier as the `against` party. - The offsetting credit is the supplier's payable entry. - This happens in the `else` branch of `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries`, in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. That branch is taken because the `update_stock and auto_accounting_for_stock` condition is false. - A code comment in that branch gives the reason: the Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at the standard cost, and it also booked the PPV. Booking PPV again on the invoice would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. - The invoice therefore only clears SRBNB at the billed amount against the supplier. - An exchange-rate-difference entry can still follow if the invoice's conversion rate differs from the receipt's. That is a separate case and not specific to Standard Cost. **Update Stock on.** This takes the first branch, which runs `make_stock_adjustment_entry` for stock items. - The invoice creates its own Stock Ledger Entries. The expense account is debited with `warehouse_debit_amount`, which is the valuation rate × qty × conversion factor. If there is no `from_warehouse`, that is the debit amount. - If the amount computed from the valuation rate differs from the Stock Ledger Entry's `stock_value_difference`, an extra "Stock Adjustment" debit is posted. For a Standard Cost item this goes to the Purchase Price Variance account, chosen by `get_stock_variance_account`. That function calls `get_purchase_price_variance_account` for Standard Cost items. For other items it uses the company's `default_expense_account`. - The same adjustment is posted for a return without `return_against`, where the stock amount is compared to the Stock Ledger Entry value. - Landed cost voucher credits and the subcontracting raw-material credit (`rm_supp_cost`) are also posted in this branch. **Difference.** With Update Stock off, the invoice only clears SRBNB and creates the payable. The receipt has already recognised the PPV. With Update Stock on, the invoice moves stock itself, so it can post its own PPV adjustment for the gap between the purchase price and the standard cost, in addition to the expense debit.

final answer
**Update Stock off** The code is in `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer`, in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`. It is the item-GL method that loops over `doc.items`, and I did not confirm that method's name. For each stock item, the `else` branch (around lines 319–350) runs because `doc.update_stock` is false. - **Expense-side entry:** The invoice posts one debit per item. It goes to `item.expense_account`, for `base_net_amount`, against the supplier. For a Purchase Receipt-linked stock item, that account is Stock Received But Not Billed (SRBNB). - **Offsetting credit:** The credit to Creditors comes from the payable entry, not this loop. - **Purchase Price Variance:** The invoice posts no PPV entry. The code comment at lines 332–336 says why. The linked Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at the standard cost and booked the Purchase Price Variance. Booking PPV again would double count it and leave SRBNB partly uncleared. - **Other entries in this branch:** There is a possible exchange-rate-difference pair. It debits the expense account and credits the exchange gain/loss account. It applies only when the Purchase Receipt's exchange rate differs from the invoice's, `net_rate` matches, and `adjust_incoming_rate` is off. There can also be a SRBNB reversal for valuation taxes. **Update Stock on** The `doc.update_stock and doc.auto_accounting_for_stock` branch runs, as the invoice itself moves stock (around lines 180–317). - **Stock Adjustment call:** `make_stock_adjustment_entry` is called first. It compares two amounts: - The amount the invoice implies, `valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. - The stock value the Stock Ledger Entry actually posted, from `voucher_wise_stock_value`. - **Variance debit:** When the two differ, it debits a variance account for the difference. The remark is "Stock Adjustment" and the entry is against `item.expense_account`. This applies to normal purchases and to returns without `return_against`. - **Which variance account:** `get_stock_variance_account` (lines 523–533) picks it. If `get_valuation_method(item, company) == "Standard Cost"`, it returns `get_purchase_price_variance_account` from `item_standard_cost`, which is the PPV account. Otherwise it returns the company's `default_expense_account`. - **Expense debit:** The branch then debits `item.expense_account` for `warehouse_debit_amount`, the stock-ledger-based amount. This applies when it is not an internal transfer. For other items, this account is typically the stock account or SRBNB. - **Extra entries:** If the item has a from-warehouse, it posts the inventory-account debit and the negative-debit entry on the source warehouse's account. If the item has landed-cost entries, it credits those accounts. If `rm_supp_cost` is set (subcontracting), it credits the supplier warehouse account. **Difference** With Update Stock off, the invoice only debits `expense_account` (SRBNB) for the full billed amount and posts no PPV, because the Purchase Receipt already booked PPV. With Update Stock on, the invoice moves stock itself. It debits `expense_account` for the stock-ledger amount. It also posts a separate "Stock Adjustment" debit to the Purchase Price Variance account for Standard Cost items, where other items use the default expense account.

final answer
**Update Stock off** The invoice posts a single expense-side line per item. The logic is in `PurchaseInvoiceGLComposer.make_item_gl_entries`, in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/purchase_invoice/services/gl_composer.py`, in the final `else:` branch. - **Debit:** `item.expense_account`. With perpetual inventory and a linked Purchase Receipt, this is normally Stock Received But Not Billed. For a deferred-expense item it is `deferred_expense_account`, unless the invoice is a return. The amount is the full billed amount, `base_amount` from `tax_service.get_amount_and_base_amount`, with `against = supplier`. - **Credit:** the supplier payable, posted separately by the payable-entry code. Taxes are posted separately by `make_tax_gl_entries`. - **No Purchase Price Variance line.** The code comment says so directly: the Purchase Receipt already booked the stock at standard cost and the PPV. The invoice only clears Stock Received But Not Billed at the billed amount. Booking PPV again would double count it and leave Stock Received But Not Billed partly uncleared. - **Inventory:** no inventory account line and no stock ledger entries are posted by the invoice. - **Optional extras:** - **Provisional accounting:** if enabled for non-stock items, `make_provisional_gl_entry` reverses the provisional entry booked in the Purchase Receipt. - **Exchange rate:** if the exchange rate differs from the Purchase Receipt's, there is an extra debit to the expense account and a matching credit to the exchange gain/loss account. **Update Stock on** This case applies when `update_stock` and `auto_accounting_for_stock` are both set and the item is a stock item or a fixed asset. The invoice then acts as the receipt itself, so it creates stock ledger entries and books inventory. - **`make_stock_adjustment_entry`:** it computes `warehouse_debit_amount = valuation_rate × qty × conversion_factor`. The `expense_account` is then debited with that stock value (`warehouse_debit_amount`), not the billed amount. The invoice's inventory posting uses `warehouse_debit_amount` as its stock-value basis. - **Variance line:** if that stock value differs from the stock ledger entry's `stock_value_difference`, a separate debit goes to the variance account. For Standard Cost items, `get_stock_variance_account` returns the Purchase Price Variance account from `get_purchase_price_variance_account`. For other items it returns the company's default expense account. The same function handles returns. - **Other lines:** these can include a `from_warehouse` transfer pair, credits for landed cost voucher amounts, and a credit to the supplier warehouse account for subcontracting raw-material cost. **Difference:** with Update Stock off, the invoice only clears the receipt's accrual (Stock Received But Not Billed) at the billed amount, and PPV stays with the Purchase Receipt. With Update Stock on, the invoice values the stock itself, so the expense debit is at stock value and any billed-vs-standard difference goes to PPV in the invoice's own entries.
Q20Group D — Numeric/logic edge casesSeveral ERPNext queries pick a single "latest" row using ORDER BY some-date DESC with LIMIT 1. How does the codebase guarantee tho…
Several ERPNext queries pick a single "latest" row using ORDER BY some-date DESC with LIMIT 1. How does the codebase guarantee those picks are deterministic when several rows share the same date — what is the pattern, why does it matter across database engines, and where is it applied? Name concrete functions and files.

final answer
**The pattern:** ERPNext adds a secondary sort key after the date column. That key is `creation` or `name`, and it is sorted in the same DESC direction. The query then reads `ORDER BY <date> DESC, creation DESC[, name DESC] LIMIT 1`. A single date column often has duplicates, so on its own it doesn't fix an order. The tiebreaker makes the most recently created row win. Adding `name` as a third key covers the case where `creation` also ties. **Why it matters across engines:** SQL doesn't define the order of rows that tie on the sort key. MariaDB/MySQL and PostgreSQL can return different rows for the same query. The same engine can also return different rows depending on the plan, the index used, or the physical layout. Without a unique, ordered tiebreaker, `LIMIT 1` could return a different row on a different engine or on a different run. That would give inconsistent exchange rates, standard costs, valuation or last-SLE results. I didn't find a comment in the code that states this reasoning. It is inferred from the pattern. **Where it is applied** (found by grep, so this may not be every instance): - `erpnext/setup/utils.py`, `get_exchange_rate`: `order_by="date desc, name desc"` on Currency Exchange with `limit=1`. - `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, the previous-SLE lookup (the `frappe.db.sql` near line 1948): `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1 for update`. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`: - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost` uses `effective_date desc, creation desc`. - `get_item_standard_rate` uses the same ordering. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`: - Line 82 uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - Line 322 uses `posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc`. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py`: `to_date desc, creation desc`. - Tests that use the same ordering: - `stock_entry/test_stock_entry.py` - `report/stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py` - `stock_reconciliation/test_stock_reconciliation.py` **Related but not the same:** - `erpnext/accounts/report/gross_profit/gross_profit.py` orders by `posting_date DESC` then `posting_time DESC`. That is a two-column ordering of a report, with no `LIMIT 1`. - `erpnext/controllers/queries.py` pairs `idx` with `name` as a secondary key for search lists.

final answer
**The pattern.** A date isn't unique, so ERPNext never sorts on it alone before a `LIMIT 1`. It extends the `ORDER BY` with tie-breakers until the order is total: `ORDER BY <date> DESC, creation DESC[, name DESC]` `creation` is the row's insert timestamp. `name` is the primary key, so it is unique and always settles the order. For queries that rank by preference first, the key goes last instead. An example is `.orderby(address.name)`. **Why it matters across engines.** SQL doesn't define which row comes first among rows that tie on the sort key. MariaDB/InnoDB tends to return them in index or primary-key order. Postgres can return them in any order, depending on the plan and on heap order. So with `ORDER BY posting_date DESC LIMIT 1`, the same data can give different rows, and different incoming rates or valuation rates, on the two engines. That makes results flaky on Postgres CI. Making the order total gives the same winner on both engines. Several code comments say this directly, for example "identical on both engines" and "deterministic LIMIT-1 tie-break across engines". **Where it is applied:** - **`erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, `update_entries_after.get_incoming_value_for_serial_nos`.** This picks the latest Stock Ledger Entry for a serial number that moved to another company. It uses `.orderby(posting_date, desc).orderby(creation, desc).orderby(name, desc).limit(1)`. The test `test_incoming_value_for_transferred_serial_no_is_deterministic` in `stock/doctype/stock_ledger_entry/test_stock_ledger_entry.py` creates two same-date entries and expects the one with the larger name to win. - **`erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`, `set_stock_balance_as_per_serial_no`.** It uses `order_by="posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc"`, and a comment says it was previously "posting_date only". The same file, around line 82, uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - **`erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`, around line 1959.** A raw SQL query uses `order by posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - **`erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`, `get_item_standard_rate`.** It uses `order_by="effective_date desc, creation desc"` with `limit=1`. - **`erpnext/regional/report/irs_1099/irs_1099.py`, `get_payer_address_html` and `get_street_address_html`.** These rank by a Postal/Billing CASE and then add a final `.orderby(address.name)` or `.orderby(link.parent)`. The test is `test_street_address_prefers_postal`. - **Related fixes in the same spirit:** - `erpnext/selling/report/customer_acquisition_and_loyalty/customer_acquisition_and_loyalty.py` adds a name tie-break to the first-seen-per-customer pick. - `erpnext/crm/doctype/test_utils.py` documents a deterministic LIMIT-1 pick. - `erpnext/controllers/website_list_for_contact.py`, `rfq_transaction_list`, selects `creation` because Postgres needs `ORDER BY` expressions in the select list of a `DISTINCT` query. **Not fixed.** I found one case that still has no tie-breaker. `RepostAccountingLedger.validate_for_closed_fiscal_year` in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/repost_accounting_ledger/repost_accounting_ledger.py` uses `order_by="period_end_date desc"` with `limit=1`. That one is safe, though, because it only reads the date value (`pluck`), so a tie returns the same date whichever row wins.

final answer
**The pattern:** every "latest row" query adds a second sort key after the date column. Rows that share the date are then ordered by `creation`, which is the row's insert timestamp. When that isn't enough, `name` is added as a third key. The query reads `ORDER BY <date> DESC, creation DESC [, name DESC] LIMIT 1`. **Why it matters across engines:** SQL doesn't define the order of rows that tie on the sort key. MariaDB/MySQL and PostgreSQL can return different tied rows for the same data, depending on the query plan, indexes, and physical row order. The same engine can also return a different row from one run to the next. A bare `ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 1` could therefore return a different row depending on engine or plan. For example, it could pick a different Stock Ledger Entry and give a different `qty_after_transaction`, or pick a different standard cost. Adding `creation` makes the sort a total order, so the pick is the same on every engine. I did not find a code comment that spells out this rationale. It is my inference from the pattern. **Where it is applied** (from my search; I may not have found every site): - `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`: the raw SQL near line 1959 (the previous-SLE lookup) orders by `posting_datetime desc, creation desc limit 1 for update`. The surrounding `creation < ...` condition around line 1946 applies the same idea to the "which entry came before" filter. - `erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`: - `get_balance_qty_from_sle()` uses `order_by="posting_datetime desc, creation desc", limit=1`. - Around line 322 there is a `frappe.get_all` with `posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc`. This is the strictest form, because it adds `name` as a final tie-breaker. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`: - `ItemStandardCost.get_last_standard_cost()` orders by `effective_date desc, creation desc`. - `get_item_standard_rate()` uses the same ordering to find the cost in effect on a posting date. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py` (around line 345): `to_date desc, creation desc`. - Tests that assert the same ordering: `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/test_stock_entry.py` and `erpnext/stock/report/stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py` both use `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`.

final answer
**The pattern.** A date column is rarely unique, so `ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 1` is not a total order. Rows that share the date can come back in any order. ERPNext fixes this by appending unique tie-breaker columns to the sort, in this order: - First `creation DESC`. Where `creation` can also tie, as in the `stock_balance.py` case below, `name DESC` is added as a final unique key. - For stock ledger entries the primary key is `posting_datetime`, or `posting_date` plus `posting_time`. **Why it matters across engines.** MariaDB/MySQL and PostgreSQL break ties differently. MariaDB tends to return rows in index or insertion order. Postgres makes no promise at all, and its order can change with the plan. Without a unique last key, the same data can yield a different "latest" row on each engine, or between runs. The repo's cross-engine guard `.github/helper/postgres_compat.py` lists "ORDER BY ... LIMIT 1 tiebreakers" among the semantic divergences that static analysis can't catch. These are left to tests and review, and the tests assert identical results on both engines. Code comments say the same thing: - `stock_balance.py`: "total order so the latest SLE is picked identically on both engines (was posting_date only)". - `packed_item.py`: "name tiebreaker … pick the same warehouse/batch/serial on both engines". - `payment_entry.py`: "unique tiebreaker so PRs sharing a transaction_date allocate in the same order on both engines". **Where it's applied.** I found these by grep, so the list may not be exhaustive. - **`erpnext/stock/stock_balance.py`** - The function that reads the last Stock Ledger Entry for each bin orders by `posting_date desc, creation desc, name desc` with `limit=1`. - The same file, around line 82, uses `posting_datetime desc, creation desc`. - **`erpnext/setup/utils.py`** - The exchange-rate lookup in Currency Exchange uses `date desc, name desc`. - **`erpnext/stock/doctype/item_standard_cost/item_standard_cost.py`** - Two queries (around lines 265 and 306) use `effective_date desc, creation desc`. - **`erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_closing_entry/stock_closing_entry.py`** - Uses `to_date desc, creation desc`. - **Query-builder form** - `.orderby(x.creation, order=Order.desc)` appears in `erpnext/stock/doctype/bin/bin.py` (the SLE query), `erpnext/assets/doctype/asset/depreciation.py` and `erpnext/stock/doctype/serial_and_batch_bundle/serial_and_batch_bundle.py`. - In `serial_and_batch_bundle.py` a comment notes that `creation` is the ORDER BY tie-breaker, and that Postgres requires ORDER BY columns to appear in the select list. - **Other `name` tie-breakers** - `erpnext/selling/report/customer_acquisition_and_loyalty/customer_acquisition_and_loyalty.py` uses a name tie-break for first-seen-per-customer. - `erpnext/selling/page/point_of_sale/point_of_sale.py`, around line 467, has a contact tie-breaker. - `erpnext/stock/doctype/packed_item/packed_item.py` and `erpnext/accounts/doctype/payment_entry/payment_entry.py` use `name` as described above. - **Tests** - `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_ledger_entry/test_stock_ledger_entry.py::test_incoming_value_for_transferred_serial_no_is_deterministic` creates SLEs that tie and checks that the larger `name` wins on both engines. - `erpnext/crm/doctype/test_utils.py` has a similar test for a LIMIT-1 pick. - Other tests use the same `creation desc` ordering: `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/test_stock_entry.py`, `erpnext/stock/report/stock_balance/test_stock_balance.py` and `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_reconciliation/test_stock_reconciliation.py`.

On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses advisory locks to serialize work on the same (item, warehouse). The locks are in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py` and `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`. Row-level `FOR UPDATE` locks stay in place for correctness. MariaDB gets the same effect from gap locks, so it doesn't need these. I read the code but didn't run any of it. **1. Repost-vs-repost gate: `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `stock_ledger.py`** - It returns `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. That is a session-level lock used as a context manager. - `repost_future_sle` wraps each `update_entries_after(...)` call in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):`. Two background Repost Item Valuation jobs on the same pair therefore take turns instead of taking inner row locks in opposite order. - The lock key is a tuple, so a colon in an item or warehouse name can't make two different pairs share one lock. - `REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT = 300` seconds, well under the 1800s job timeout. A worker that times out raises `QueryTimeoutError`, which `repost_item_valuation.py` lists in `RecoverableErrors` along with `QueryDeadlockError` and `JobTimeoutException`. The job is re-queued rather than marked Failed. - On MariaDB it returns `nullcontext()`. If the frappe version has no `advisory_lock`, `hasattr` makes it fall back to no gate. - The synchronous `repost_current_voucher` submit path is deliberately not gated, so a submit never blocks behind a background repost. It relies on the existing deadlock retry. **2. Stock-write gate: `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `stock_ledger.py`** - It calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=...)`. The lock lasts for the transaction, is re-entrant, and is released at commit or rollback. - Postgres locking reads can't see rows another transaction is still inserting. Without this gate, two writers would compute from the same stale previous Stock Ledger Entry, and one Bin write would be lost. - `make_sl_entries` calls it for `sorted({(item_code, warehouse)})` pairs. The sort keeps two vouchers from taking the locks in opposite order. - `update_entries_after.__init__` also calls it. **3. Batch average-rate gate: `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate` in `serial_batch_bundle.py`** - For outward entries (`actual_qty <= 0`) on Postgres, it first calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", item_code, warehouse))`. - Only after that does it read the batch history in `get_batch_stock_before_date` and compute the average rate. Two jobs therefore can't both compute the rate from the same history for that pair. - A comment in the code says row-locking the whole history would write a lock marker on every row, so the advisory lock is used instead. - Inward entries just use the bundle's `total_amount` and need no lock. **Related Postgres handling** - `serial_and_batch_bundle.py` still uses `.for_update()` when picking batches (lines 1707, 1737 and 2505). - `stock_reservation_entry.py` and `pick_list.py` use `FOR UPDATE` on parent rows or `transaction_advisory_lock(("pick-allocate", item_code))`, because Postgres has no gap locks. - Tests for the gates are `test_stock_write_takes_sle_advisory_gate` and `test_outward_batch_valuation_takes_transaction_advisory_lock`. The repost deadlock-retry test is in `test_repost_item_valuation.py`.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses three separate advisory locks, each keyed on the item and warehouse. They sit on top of the existing row locks (`FOR UPDATE`) and the deadlock-retry logic. MariaDB doesn't use them. It relies on gap locks from its `FOR UPDATE` reads instead. I read the code and did not run it. Of the three, the first two address deadlocks and the third addresses double-computation. 1. **Repost-vs-repost gate: `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`.** - It takes a session-level lock, `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. The timeout is 300 seconds. - `repost_future_sle` wraps its work in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):` (around line 335). The lock is taken before the inner `... for update` row locks, so two background reposts of the same pair take turns. Without it they could acquire row locks in different orders and deadlock. - Row locks still enforce correctness. This lock only reduces deadlock and retry churn. - It deliberately does not gate the synchronous `repost_current_voucher` submit path, because blocking a submit behind a background repost would be worse. That path keeps relying on the existing deadlock retry. - If `frappe.db` has no `advisory_lock`, it falls back to `nullcontext()`. 2. **All stock writes: `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in the same file.** - It calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. - The lock is scoped to the transaction. It is re-entrant and is released at commit or rollback. - It exists because PostgreSQL locking reads can't see rows that another transaction is still inserting. Without the gate, two writers could both compute from the same stale previous SLE, and the loser's Bin write would be lost. MariaDB's gap locks prevent this. 3. **Batch average rate: `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`.** - For outward entries (`actual_qty <= 0`) on PostgreSQL, it first calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. Only then does it call `get_batch_stock_before_date()`, which sums the stock value difference and quantity per batch. - This stops two jobs from reading the same history and both computing the average rate for that item-warehouse. - `get_batch_stock_before_date` skips `.for_update()` when `db_type == "postgres"`. PostgreSQL rejects `FOR UPDATE` together with `GROUP BY`. Row-locking the whole history would also write a lock marker on every tuple. - The same pattern appears in the legacy valuation queries in `erpnext/stock/deprecated_serial_batch.py` (around lines 147, 268 and 396). Their comments say the caller `calculate_avg_rate` serializes on the lock instead. **Related locks:** - `get_future_stock_vouchers` in `erpnext/accounts/utils.py` handles the same `GROUP BY` problem for reposting. On PostgreSQL it first locks the matching future SLE rows with a separate `SELECT 1 ... FOR UPDATE` pass, then runs the grouped query without `FOR UPDATE`. The row locks last until the transaction ends. - `PickList.set_item_locations` takes a `("pick-allocate", item_code)` transaction lock, with the item codes sorted so overlapping documents can't deadlock.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext uses advisory locks keyed on the item and warehouse. They serialize the jobs, and a retry path covers any deadlock that still occurs. I read the code in `stock_ledger.py`, `serial_batch_bundle.py` and `repost_item_valuation.py`. I did not run anything. **1. `repost_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in `erpnext/stock/stock_ledger.py`** - `repost_future_sle()` wraps each `update_entries_after(...)` call in `with repost_gate(item_code, warehouse):`. - On Postgres, the gate takes a session-level `frappe.db.advisory_lock(("stock_repost", item_code, warehouse), timeout=REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT)`. - It is taken before the inner `... for update` row locks. Two background reposts of the same (item, warehouse) therefore take turns instead of locking rows in conflicting order. - The key is a tuple, so a colon inside an item code or warehouse name can't make two different pairs share one lock. - `REPOST_LOCK_TIMEOUT` is 300 seconds, well under the 1800-second job timeout. A job that waits too long raises a recoverable `QueryTimeoutError` and re-queues. - Only repost-vs-repost is gated. The synchronous submit path (`repost_current_voucher`) is deliberately left ungated, so a submit never blocks behind a background job. It relies on the deadlock retry instead. - On MariaDB, or on a frappe version without `advisory_lock`, it returns `nullcontext()`. MariaDB keeps the plain deadlock-retry path. **2. `sle_processing_gate(item_code, warehouse)` in the same file** - On Postgres it takes a transaction-scoped, re-entrant `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("stock-sle", item_code, warehouse))`. It is released at commit or rollback. - MariaDB gets this serialization from gap locks. Postgres has none, and its locking reads can't see rows another transaction is inserting. - Without the gate, two writers would compute from the same stale previous Stock Ledger Entry, and the loser's Bin write would be lost. **3. The batch average rate in `serial_batch_bundle.py`** - Inside `calculate_avg_rate()` (the batch valuation class), the outgoing branch takes `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", item_code, warehouse))` on Postgres. It does this before calling `get_batch_stock_before_date()`. - That query is a `GROUP BY batch_no` sum of `stock_value_difference` and `qty`. `FOR UPDATE` is invalid with `GROUP BY` on Postgres, and row-locking the whole history would write a lock marker on every row. So `for_update()` is applied only when `db_type != "postgres"`. - Because the lock is held until the transaction ends, concurrent jobs can't read the same history and each compute the same batch average rate. **4. Deadlock retry in `repost_item_valuation.py`** - `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)`. `repost()` classifies errors by exception type, so a Postgres "deadlock detected" re-queues the record as "In Progress". The old string match only recognised MariaDB's "Deadlock found" and would have marked Postgres deadlocks as permanently Failed. - `deduplicate_similar_repost()` and `skip_reposts_covered_by_dependents()` also reduce overlapping reposts. Row-level `for_update` locks still enforce correctness. The advisory locks mainly cut deadlocks and wasted retries.

final answer
On PostgreSQL, ERPNext serializes concurrent batch valuations with a transaction-scoped advisory lock. If a deadlock still happens, the Repost Item Valuation job retries it. **Advisory lock (the main guard)** - The lock is in `BatchNoValuation.calculate_avg_rate` in `erpnext/stock/serial_batch_bundle.py`, around lines 818–845. - It applies only to outward entries, where `sle.actual_qty <= 0`. These are the entries that must compute an average rate from the batch history. - On postgres, before reading that history, it calls `frappe.db.transaction_advisory_lock(("batch-valuation", sle.item_code, sle.warehouse))`. - The key is the `(item, warehouse)` pair, so two jobs working on the same item-warehouse queue up. Jobs on different pairs run in parallel. - The lock is held until commit or rollback. That means the second job reads the history only after the first job's rows are committed, so the average rate is computed once against consistent data and is not double-computed or computed from stale reads. - Inward entries (`actual_qty > 0`) take `total_amount` from the Serial and Batch Bundle, so they need no lock. **Why the code avoids row locks on postgres** - `get_batch_stock_before_date` builds a grouped query over `Serial and Batch Entry`. It sums `stock_value_difference` and `qty`, grouped by `batch_no`. - On MariaDB the query ends with `.for_update()`. Its row locks and gap locks serialize concurrent jobs. - On postgres the code skips `for_update()` (`if frappe.db.db_type != "postgres"`). `FOR UPDATE` is invalid with `GROUP BY` there. Postgres also has no gap locks. Row-locking the whole history would write a lock marker on every tuple, which is expensive and invites deadlocks. - The advisory lock gives the same serialization without either problem. **Deadlock retry in the reposting job** - `erpnext/stock/doctype/repost_item_valuation/repost_item_valuation.py` defines `RecoverableErrors = (JobTimeoutException, QueryDeadlockError, QueryTimeoutError)`. - `repost()` refers to this tuple. I did not read the handler body, so I haven't confirmed exactly what it does with these errors. These are the errors it treats as recoverable. - `repost()` also calls `skip_reposts_covered_by_dependents`, `set_status` and `notify_error_to_stock_managers`. I did not read these, so I can't say how they deduplicate or resolve overlapping reposts.

When a Period Closing Voucher is submitted and its GL entry processing is split across multiple background workers, how does the system ensure that two workers never pick up and process the same date/report-type row at the same time?

final answer
Two workers can't pick up the same date/report-type row because a row is claimed in two steps. The claim is a locked read of a `Queued` row, then a status change to `Running`. A worker also re-checks that status before it does any work. All of this is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. I read the code but didn't run it, so this is from the source, not from a test. The work is tracked as rows in the child table `Process Period Closing Voucher Detail`. Each row has a `processing_date`, a `report_type` and a `status` of Queued, Running, Completed, Paused or Cancelled. 1. **Locked read.** `initialize_parallel_threads(docname)` and `schedule_next_date(docname)` both select rows with `status == "Queued"`. The query ends in `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`, which is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. - A selecting transaction takes a row lock on the rows it gets. - A second dispatcher running at the same moment skips rows that are already locked, so it can't get the same row. It takes the next `Queued` row or none. - `initialize_parallel_threads` claims up to 4 rows at once (`threads = 4`). `schedule_next_date` claims 1. 2. **Claim by status.** While it holds the lock, the dispatcher sets each selected row to `status = "Running"` with `frappe.db.set_value`. It then calls `frappe.db.commit()` (skipped under test). The commit releases the lock and makes `Running` visible to others, so later queries filtering on `Queued` no longer return that row. The commit comes before `frappe.enqueue(..., enqueue_after_commit=True)`. The code comments say this keeps the transaction short, which avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. 3. **Worker guard.** `process_individual_date(docname, row_name, date, report_type, parentfield)` starts by reading the row's current status. If it isn't `"Running"`, it returns immediately. This stops a worker from running a row that was paused, cancelled or already completed in the meantime. 4. **Finish and chain.** The worker writes `closing_balance` and sets the row to `Completed`. It commits and then calls `schedule_next_date(docname)` to claim the next `Queued` row, with the same locking. 5. **Summary step.** When no `Queued` rows are left and every row is `Completed`, `schedule_next_date` enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries`. It first checks `is_job_running(f"summarize_{docname}")`, so the summary job also isn't enqueued twice. `pause_pcv_processing`, `cancel_pcv_processing` and `resume_pcv_processing` change row statuses. Resume sets Paused rows back to `Queued`.

final answer
Two workers never pick up the same date/report-type row, because rows are claimed with a row lock and a status flag, and each job checks that flag before it runs. All of this is in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. **How the claim works** 1. **Setup.** Submitting a Process Period Closing Voucher calls `on_submit`, which calls `start_pcv_processing`. That calls `initialize_parallel_threads`. Each processing date becomes two child rows in `normal_balances`, one for "Profit and Loss" and one for "Balance Sheet". The child doctype is Process Period Closing Voucher Detail, and every row starts as `Queued`. 2. **Locking and claiming.** `initialize_parallel_threads` selects up to 4 `Queued` rows with `.limit(4).for_update(skip_locked=True)`. This is `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. A row locked by one transaction is skipped, not waited on, by any other transaction running the same query. For each selected row it sets `status = "Running"` and enqueues `process_individual_date` on the `long` queue. 3. **Commit before the job starts.** The code commits (`frappe.db.commit()`, skipped in tests), and the jobs use `enqueue_after_commit=True`. So the `Running` status is saved before any worker can start the job. Anyone who looks later no longer sees those rows as `Queued`. 4. **Chaining.** When a worker finishes a row, `process_individual_date` calls `schedule_next_date`. That function uses the same `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` query with `limit(1)`, marks the row `Running`, commits, and enqueues the next job. Two workers that finish at the same moment therefore lock different `Queued` rows, or skip a locked one. 5. **Guard in the worker.** `process_individual_date` starts by reading the row's `status`. If it isn't `Running`, it returns immediately. This also covers a row that was paused or cancelled after it was queued: `pause_pcv_processing` flips `Queued` and `Running` rows to `Paused`. 6. **Finishing.** The worker writes `closing_balance`, sets the row to `Completed`, and commits. Only then does it call `schedule_next_date`. Once no `Queued` rows remain and every row is `Completed`, `schedule_next_date` enqueues `summarize_and_post_ledger_entries`. It first checks `is_job_running` for the job name `summarize_<docname>`, so only one summarize job runs. The comments in the code say the transactions on the parent and detail rows are kept short to avoid REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. I read the code but did not run it or test the concurrency behavior.

final answer
Two workers never pick up the same date/report-type row because each row is claimed with a database row lock and a status flag. Both are in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. I didn't read the code that runs after `schedule_next_date` sets a row to "Running" (about line 279 onward). I also didn't run anything. The work is tracked in the child table "Process Period Closing Voucher Detail". Each row has a `processing_date`, a `report_type` and a `status` of Queued, Running, Paused, Completed or Cancelled. **How a row is claimed** - `initialize_parallel_threads(docname)` and `schedule_next_date(docname)` both run the same query. It selects detail rows where `parent = docname` and `status = "Queued"`. - The query is ordered by `parentfield, idx, processing_date`. It is limited to 4 rows (the `threads` value) in `initialize_parallel_threads` and to 1 row in `schedule_next_date`. - It ends with `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`, which is `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. If another transaction already holds a row lock, that row is skipped rather than waited on. A second worker therefore gets different Queued rows, or none. - Right after the select, each chosen row is set to `status = "Running"`. Only then is `process_individual_date` enqueued (on the "long" queue, with `enqueue_after_commit=True`). The status change is what stops later selections from picking the row up again, because they only look at "Queued" rows. - The code then calls `frappe.db.commit()`, except in tests. The comment says this keeps the transaction on the voucher and detail rows short, which avoids concurrency errors under REPEATABLE READ. The commit also releases the row locks, but by then the rows are already "Running". **Related behaviour** - `start_pcv_processing` only starts when the parent status is "Queued" or "Running". `initialize_parallel_threads` sets the parent to "Running". - `pause_pcv_processing` moves Queued and Running rows to "Paused". `resume_pcv_processing` moves Paused rows back to "Queued" and restarts. If nothing is paused, it calls `schedule_next_date` to unstick a parent that is stuck in "Running". - `cancel_pcv_processing` sets only Queued rows to "Cancelled".

final answer
Two mechanisms work together: a row-level lock on the claim query, and a status flag on each row. Both are in `erpnext/accounts/doctype/process_period_closing_voucher/process_period_closing_voucher.py`. **1. Claiming a row uses `SELECT … FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`.** - On submit, `PeriodClosingVoucher.on_submit` creates a Process Period Closing Voucher (PPCV). Its `on_submit` calls `start_pcv_processing`, which calls `initialize_parallel_threads`. - The PPCV's child table, Process Period Closing Voucher Detail (PPCVD), holds one row per `processing_date` and `report_type`. The rows are in `normal_balances` and `z_opening_balances`, and each starts as `Queued`. - `initialize_parallel_threads` selects up to 4 `Queued` rows with `.for_update(skip_locked=True)`. `schedule_next_date` runs the same kind of query with `limit(1)`. - A row locked by one transaction is skipped by the others, not waited on. So two workers can't select the same row, and there is no blocking. **2. The claim is recorded by setting the row to `Running`.** - Right after selecting a row, the code sets its `status` to `Running`. Only then does it call `frappe.enqueue(process_individual_date, …, row_name=…)`. - The `enqueue` call uses `enqueue_after_commit=True`, and the code commits before the job is queued. The `Running` status is therefore visible to other transactions before the job can run. - Any later claim query filters on `status = "Queued"`, so it never sees that row again. - `process_individual_date` also re-checks the row. If its status isn't `Running` (for example it was paused or cancelled), it returns without doing anything. **3. Chained scheduling and short transactions.** - When `process_individual_date` finishes a row, it saves `closing_balance`, sets the row to `Completed` and commits. It then calls `schedule_next_date(docname)`, which claims the next `Queued` row with the same locked select. - The code commits right after claiming (`frappe.db.commit()` outside tests). The comments say this keeps the PPCV and PPCVD transactions short and avoids REPEATABLE READ concurrency errors. It also releases the row lock once the `Running` flag is durable. **Pause and cancel.** - `pause_pcv_processing` sets both `Queued` and stuck `Running` rows to `Paused`. A stuck `Running` row can then be picked up again on resume. - `cancel_pcv_processing` sets `Queued` rows to `Cancelled`.

final answer
**Overview.** A Job Card's "Make Stock Entry" button calls `make_stock_entry` in `erpnext/manufacturing/doctype/job_card/mapper.py`. That function builds a "Material Transfer for Manufacture" Stock Entry. At the end of `set_missing_values`, it loops over the items and calls `set_previous_operation_serial_batch(target, item)`. That function lives in `erpnext/stock/doctype/stock_entry/services/manufacturing.py`, and it does the pre-fill. **Guards.** `set_previous_operation_serial_batch` returns without doing anything in these cases: - The Stock Entry has no `work_order`. - The row has no `s_warehouse`. - The row's quantity is 0 or less. The quantity used is `qty × conversion_factor`. - The row already has a `serial_and_batch_bundle`, `serial_no` or `batch_no`. **How the pool is chosen.** `get_previous_operation_output_sn_batch(work_order, item_code, warehouse)` builds the pool of available serials and batches. 1. It only applies to items that are the `finished_good` of some Work Order Operation in that Work Order. Ordinary raw materials get an empty result. The item must also have `has_serial_no` or `has_batch_no`. 2. It collects what earlier operations produced. `_get_operation_sn_batch(..., produced=True)` reads submitted Stock Entries of the same Work Order. They must be purpose "Manufacture", with `is_finished_item=1` and `t_warehouse` equal to the row's source warehouse. 3. It reads the Serial and Batch Entry rows from those entries' bundles, ordered by bundle and idx. Cancelled entries are excluded. 4. It collects what was already taken out. `produced=False` reads the same Work Order's submitted entries where the item left that warehouse (`s_warehouse` equal to the row's warehouse). 5. Serial numbers in the produced set but not in the consumed set stay in the pool. For batches, the available quantity is produced minus consumed, kept only if it is above 0. **How the pool is trimmed.** `_cap_pool_to_qty(pool, qty)` trims it to the row quantity. - For serial-tracked items, it takes the first `int(qty)` serials from the pool. - For batch-tracked items, it walks the batches in order and takes `min(batch_qty, remaining)` from each until the quantity is covered. **What gets created.** If any serials or batches were selected, `SerialBatchCreation(...).make_serial_and_batch_bundle()` creates an Outward Serial and Batch Bundle. The bundle is created with `do_not_submit=True`, so it stays a draft. It is set on `row.serial_and_batch_bundle`, and `use_serial_batch_fields` is set to 0. **If fewer units are available than the row quantity.** - It does not throw an error. - The row gets a partial bundle holding only what is available. For example, 3 serials against a row quantity of 5, or batches totalling less than the quantity. - The row's `qty` stays unchanged, and the bundle's `qty` is passed as the full `qty`. - The shortfall is left for the user to fill in manually. - If nothing is available, no bundle is created and the row stays blank. I did not check what validation runs when the incomplete entry is saved or submitted.

final answer
When a Job Card is turned into a Stock Entry, the pre-fill is decided by `set_previous_operation_serial_batch`. If fewer units are available than the row's quantity, the row is filled with what exists and the shortfall is left blank. I read the code but did not run it. **Where it starts** `make_stock_entry` in `manufacturing/doctype/job_card/mapper.py` builds a "Material Transfer for Manufacture" Stock Entry from the Job Card. In its `set_missing_values` step it loops over every item row and calls `set_previous_operation_serial_batch(target, item)`. That function lives in `stock/doctype/stock_entry/services/manufacturing.py`. **When the row is skipped** The function does nothing in any of these cases: - The Stock Entry has no `work_order`. - The row has no source warehouse (`s_warehouse`). - The row's quantity, multiplied by its conversion factor, is zero or less. - The row already has a `serial_and_batch_bundle`, `serial_no` or `batch_no`. **How the pool of serials or batches is chosen** `get_previous_operation_output_sn_batch(work_order, item_code, warehouse)` builds the pool. It returns an empty pool unless all of these hold: - A Work Order Operation row of that Work Order lists the item as its `finished_good`, so only semi-finished goods qualify and ordinary raw materials are ignored. - The item has serial numbers or batches. For a qualifying item, it works out what is still available in that warehouse: - **Produced:** serials and batches from submitted Stock Entries of the same Work Order with purpose "Manufacture". The row must be a finished item (`is_finished_item=1`) going into that `t_warehouse`. - **Consumed:** serials and batches from submitted Stock Entries of the same Work Order that took the item out of that `s_warehouse`. - **Serial numbers:** each produced serial is kept only if it was not consumed. - **Batches:** the available quantity is produced minus consumed, kept only if it is above zero. `_get_operation_bundles` finds the bundles and `_get_operation_sn_batch` reads the Serial and Batch Entry rows. Entries that are cancelled (`is_cancelled=1`) are ignored. **How the pool is trimmed and attached** `_cap_pool_to_qty(pool, qty)` trims the pool to the row's quantity: - **Serial items:** it takes the first `int(qty)` serials from the pool. - **Batch items:** it takes batches in order, using `min(batch_qty, remaining)` from each, until the quantity is covered. The function then creates a draft Outward Serial and Batch Bundle through `SerialBatchCreation(...).make_serial_and_batch_bundle()` with `do_not_submit=True`. It sets `row.serial_and_batch_bundle` to that bundle and sets `use_serial_batch_fields = 0`. **When fewer units are available than the row's quantity** - **No error:** nothing is thrown. - **Partial fill:** the bundle holds only the serials or batch quantities that are available. The row's `qty` is not reduced. - **Nothing available:** if the pool is empty, no bundle is created and the row stays blank. - **Left to the user:** the docstring says the shortfall is "left blank for the user". They have to add the missing serials or batches, or adjust the quantity, before submitting. - **Caught later:** the normal serial/batch quantity validation on the Stock Entry should flag any mismatch at that point. I did not trace that validation.
